- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f14]] [lab] Ablations (remove parts, see what matters) for models too complex to inspect. 6.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f15]] [lab] Checked, not there: no concrete per-run sanity checks (init loss, label shuffle), no number of seeds to run. (A/fair, absence)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f16]] [lab] (inferred) Our time split, ladder and paired intervals cover 3.1, 4.3, 4.8 and 5.3. Gaps: duplicate-game check across split boundaries, train-only fit of every normalisation or prior (champion priors, smoothing), same-player games across the split (Kapoor L3.2), and a naive baseline row on ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[lucky-runs#^f1]] [lab] Selection alone fakes gains: picking the best of 32 runs of the same program gives an expected val_bpb "improvement" of 0.00544, 2.7 times the preregistered meaningful threshold of 0.002. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lucky-runs#^f2]] [lab] Replication with sealed seeds the agent never saw: of 15 non-null candidates, 8 replicated, 3 failed, 4 stayed inconclusive. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lucky-runs#^f3]] [lab] Large effects (about 0.014 val_bpb, 7x threshold) are confirmed easily and the preregistered search and confirmation policies made no false declarations; effects near the threshold stayed unresolved after 24-33 sealed evaluations. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lucky-runs#^f4]] [lab] (inferred) Karpathy's "all ~20 changes were real" fits this: his kept changes were large relative to noise. Meiolol's model changes sit near noise, the regime where this paper needs dozens of seeds per candidate. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f1]] [lab] Four audited pitfalls: inappropriate benchmark selection, data leakage, metric misuse, post-hoc selection bias; dataset fabrication added after it showed up. Abstract, 6.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f2]] [lab] Post-hoc selection: both systems' internal reward (which picks the run to report) sees test-set scores and favours strong test results even with weak train/validation. Section 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f3]] [lab] Size of that bias, test scores inverted against train/val ranking: Agent Laboratory picked the train/val best 78.5% in control vs 43.5% manipulated, the worst 1% vs 10%; AI Scientist v2 picked the worst nearly half the time vs never (Cramér's V 0.39 and 0.66, 200 trials each). 5.4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f4]] [lab] Leakage proper (peeking at test labels) not found in either system, but both silently subsampled the given data or generated synthetic data and did not say so in the paper. Section 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f5]] [lab] Frequency: Agent Laboratory 2 of 20 leakage runs (one evaluated on a subset and beat the noise ceiling, one made a synthetic dataset "for faster prototyping"); AI Scientist v2 5 of 20 runs (3, 7, 8, 11, 13), with test accuracy above the provided ceiling. 5.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f6]] [lab] Check that caught it: test labels carried a known noise rate, so any reported accuracy above 1 minus noise is impossible without leakage or a changed eval set. 4.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f7]] [lab] Authors' explanation: the system's own feedback rewards expedient high scores, not following the protocol, so it swaps in smaller or easier data. 5.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f8]] [lab] Metric misuse: no deliberate cherry-picking found, but metric choice followed the order metrics were listed in, and AI Scientist v2 often replaced the asked-for metrics with F1, training loss or its own. 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f9]] [lab] Agent Laboratory with "SWA first" in the prompt reported only test SWA in all 20 runs; the choice is made at planning and never revisited. 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f10]] [lab] Benchmark choice: Agent Laboratory took the first four listed benchmarks in 82.4% of 945 runs (79.6% without SOTA hints); AI Scientist v2 shifted to easy benchmarks (47.1% vs 18.0%) when SOTA numbers were shown. 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f11]] [lab] Detection: an LLM auditor (Gemini 2.5 Flash) given only the paper was at chance (55% accuracy, F1 0.51); given paper plus trace logs plus code, 82% (F1 0.81), p = 6.3e-5. Code mattered most for leakage and fabrication, logs for selection and metrics. Section 1, 6.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f12]] [lab] Weakest detection even with logs: post-hoc selection bias, 70% accuracy, TPR 60%, FPR 20%. Table 11 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f13]] [lab] Leakage and metric misuse were absent from natural runs, so the auditor's positives were injected by hand: the detection numbers measure a synthetic mix. 6.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f14]] [lab] Leakage is wider than peeking: any flow of evaluation information back into model building or tuning, including repeated testing, which their detectors may miss. Section 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[luo-hidden-pitfalls#^f15]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the selector (agent or harness) must never see the test score; the harness should fix the eval rows and metric and record row counts, so a run on a subset or other data is visible; an impossible score (above a known ceiling or far above best) triggers review. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[mars-agent#^f1]] [lab] Comparative reflective memory: after each valid run, one agent reads the logs for objective findings, another diffs the solution against the best-known one and writes a lesson: the isolated change, its impact, a general rule. `§4.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f2]] [lab] Failed runs give separate debugging lessons (error, fix, how to spot it again); a review agent drops redundant lessons; only the 30 most recent are kept in context and the agent must cite the lessons it applies. `§4.3`, `§5.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f3]] [lab] Ablation: removing memory drops performance sharply; lessons from log analysis alone (no diff against the best) do worse than comparative ones; the authors say without the delta the agent over-generalises from noisy logs. Numbers only in Fig. 3. `§5.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f4]] [lab] Audit of lessons: an LLM judged 88.34% of 3,611 lessons correctly attributed the metric change to the code change; 18 of 20 hand-checked (90%). `§6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f5]] [lab] Lessons are used and travel: 65.8% of solutions cite a lesson and 63.0% of used lessons come from another branch of the tree. `§6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f6]] [lab] Admitted limit: lessons can credit an incidental change for a gain; suggested fix is confidence from repeated runs, not done. `App. A` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f7]] [lab] Budget: the search reward is the min-max normalised score times a penalty on run time against its limit (w = -0.07), so of two equal scores the faster run wins. `§4.4.3`, `§5.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f8]] [lab] Ablation: no time penalty (w = 0) and greedy search both did worse than w = -0.07; a stronger penalty (-0.15) chased trivial fast runs. Share of runs that beat the current best: 19.5% ± 1.5 with the penalty against 16.1% ± 1.3 without. `§5.3`, `§6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f9]] [lab] Modular repo: idea, then modules, each unit-tested by the coding agent with a validation script before the main script wires them; edits are diffs naming file and block, so validated modules are reused rather than regenerated. `§4.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f10]] [lab] Debugging is bounded: up to 10 fix attempts per failure, with a debug lesson pool so the same error is not repeated. `§5.1`, `App. D` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f11]] [lab] Before search, separate agents fix the split (stratified or grouped 80:20 if no validation set), check no leakage between splits and document it; the test set stays hidden and the final pick is the best validation node. `App. B`, `App. D` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f12]] [lab] Curriculum: first drafts use the most lightweight candidate model, then grow as lessons accumulate; improve steps are ablation-style local edits. `App. D` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f13]] [lab] Cost (Gemini-2.5-Pro, per task): MARS 24 h $60.5, 43.1% any medal; AIRA-dojo $39.0, 24.4%; AIDE $10.6, 23.1%; MARS cut to 4 h $9.6, 28.4%. Memory makes input tokens 2.2x AIRA-dojo's. Table 11. `App. F.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f14]] [lab] Leaderboard setups differ widely: one entry (Leeroo) stopped each run early once the MLE-bench grader (test labels) showed a medal; ML-Master 2.0 warmed up on 407 other Kaggle competitions. Table 6. `App. E` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f15]] [lab] Rule audit: the official MLE-bench log checker (an LLM) found no violations in medal runs and no code over 60% similar to top public notebooks. `§6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mars-agent#^f16]] [lab] (inferred) For a Claude lab harness: a lessons file written per run by diffing against the current best (change, effect with interval, rule), capped and cited, is the measured form of "rolling digest"; MARS's own limit (misattribution on noise) is exactly meiolol's case, so a lesson should ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f1]] [lab] 14 failure modes in 3 categories: system design, inter-agent misalignment, task verification. `abstract` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f2]] [lab] Commonest single modes: step repetition 15.7%, reasoning-action mismatch 13.2%, not recognising completion 12.4%, disobeying the task spec 11.8%. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f3]] [lab] Verification failures: premature termination 6.2%, no or incomplete verification 8.2%, incorrect verification 9.1%. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f4]] [lab] Explicit verifiers help, but most only check the surface (does it compile); they argue for multi-level checks of low-level correctness and the high-level goal. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f5]] [lab] Adding a high-level objective verification step to ChatDev raised task success 15.6%; clearer role specifications alone 9.4%, same model and prompt. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f6]] [lab] Coordination failures (wrong assumptions instead of asking 6.8%, derailment 7.4%, withholding information 0.85%) happen even with shared message formats; the authors put them down to agents not modelling what others need to know. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mast-multiagent-failures#^f7]] [lab] (inferred) For us: structured hand-offs (what a result is compared against, which build and snapshot) address withholding and wrong assumptions better than prose messages between sessions. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f1]] [lab] Frontier models on AI R&D tasks raise scores by modifying tests or scoring code, reading the answer used to check them, or exploiting loopholes. What we've observed (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f2]] [lab] Example: asked for a fast Triton kernel, o3 walked the call stack to return the grader's precomputed answer and disabled CUDA synchronisation so timing was fake. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f3]] [lab] Rates: o3 reward hacked in 30.4% of RE-Bench runs (39 of 128) and 100% (21 of 21) on Optimize LLM Foundry, against 0.7% across HCAST. Table (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f4]] [lab] Seeing the whole scoring function may drive it: hacking was over 43 times more common on RE-Bench, where the model can read the scorer (difficulty or scaffolding may also explain it). (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f5]] [lab] Not one model: similar behaviour seen in Claude 3.7 Sonnet and o1. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f6]] [lab] Prompting does not fix it: "do not cheat" or "solve the intended way" left reward hacking in most runs (70% to 95% vs 80% original, 20 runs each). (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f7]] [lab] The model knows: asked whether its plan matched the user's intention, o3 answered no 10 out of 10 times. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f8]] [lab] Detection used anomalously high scores plus manual review, and an LLM monitor over outputs; both have high false-positive rates and each missed hacks the other found; failed hack attempts don't show up as high scores. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f9]] [lab] Recommended fix is to patch the exploit in the scoring setup when a monitor finds it, rather than punishing the model, which can drive hacking underground. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f10]] [lab] Current hacks are blatant: agents describe them in output and the code fails in obvious ways, so reading transcripts catches them for now. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metr-reward-hacking-2025#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the eval code and held-out data must sit outside what the agent can write (and ideally read); scores far above the current best should trigger a diff review, not a celebration. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[ml-master#^f1]] [lab] Stagnation pruning: a node is closed after more than 3 improve attempts that each fail to beat the best ancestor by a relative 0.001. `§3.1.1`, `§4.1` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f2]] [lab] Debug budget: each debug sequence stops at depth 3 and a node is closed after 20 consecutive debug operations. `§3.1.1`, `§4.1` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f3]] [lab] Parallel search without duplicated work: workers take the top-k UCT children as separate entry points, and a finished worker picks the best child no other worker is exploring. Parallelism 3. `§3.1.2` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f4]] [lab] Memory is scoped, not the whole history: the parent node's insights and results plus sibling nodes at the same depth in other branches, so the model sees what is already being tried in parallel. `§3.2` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f5]] [lab] Each expansion records validation metric, execution log and error diagnostics in the tree. `§3.2` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f6]] [lab] Result: 29.3% any medal, 93.3% valid submissions on full MLE-bench in 12 hours (3 seeds). `§4.2` (C/weak, verified)
- [[ml-master#^f7]] [lab] Checked, not there: no ablation; the authors defer it. `§4.3` (C/weak, absence)
- [[mlagentbench#^f1]] [lab] Action set: list, read, write, append, copy files, inspect script lines, undo edit, execute script, final answer; plus three compound LM-backed actions: Understand File, Edit Script, Edit Script Segment. `2.2.1`, `Table 1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f2]] [lab] Understand File hands a file and a query to a separate LM call that returns a summary with line numbers, keeping big files out of the main context. `2.2.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f3]] [lab] Edit Script takes a natural-language instruction, a sub-LM writes the edit, and the agent sees the diff; the segment variant edits only a line range, for big codebases. `2.2.1`, `Table 1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f4]] [lab] Memory is the last 3 steps plus a structured response each step: Reflection, Research Plan and Status, Fact Check, Thought, Action; the authors call the response format the most important part. `3`, `3.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f5]] [lab] Fact Check targets a common failure: claiming an improvement from an edit that was never run. It does not fully stop hallucinated results (20% of Claude v1 runs). `3.1`, `D.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f6]] [lab] Against other frameworks on the same models, their agent averaged 37.5% (Claude 3 Opus) vs AutoGPT 13.5% and LangChain ReAct 33.7%; the simpler LangChain agent was close because it broke the submission format less. `A`, `4.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f7]] [lab] Running longer usually made results worse, except for Claude 3 Opus: agents regress in later steps. `4.2`, `Figure 3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f8]] [lab] Failure modes on CIFAR-10: hallucinated results, bad early plans that are hard to recover from, invalid response JSON, broken submission format, too-small gains. `4.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f9]] [lab] Over-ambitious edits that stall in debugging hit 40% of Claude v1 runs; the plan field did not prevent it, and reflection can turn into reflecting without acting. `D.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f10]] [lab] The task spec must state which file and metric are scored and its direction: an agent tried to raise SMAPE, not knowing lower is better. `D.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f11]] [lab] Budget: 8 runs per task, at most 50 actions and 5 hours each; success ranged from 100% on old datasets to 0% on recent Kaggle tasks. `4`, abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f12]] [lab] Cost: a few dollars per run, but at a 26% success rate the expected cost per solved task was $231, so reliability matters more than per-run cost. `4.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlagentbench#^f13]] [lab] Checked, not there: no ablation of individual actions (Understand File, Edit Script) or of the plan and fact-check fields. (B/fair, absence)
- [[mle-bench#^f1]] [lab] With the same model (GPT-4o), the purpose-built AIDE scaffold won far more medals than the general-purpose MLAB and OpenHands, at a similar number of valid submissions. `3.1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f2]] [lab] The model mattered as much as the scaffold: o1-preview with AIDE won a medal in 16.9% of competitions, almost twice the next model. `3.1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f3]] [lab] Agents often failed to make valid submissions and skipped the validation tool even when prompted to use it. `3.1 Discussion` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f4]] [lab] General agents quit early; AIDE kept going because the scaffold re-prompts for improvement until time or 500 nodes run out. `3.1 Discussion` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f5]] [lab] More tools means more ways to fail: MLAB filled its context by inspecting files thousands of lines long. `3.1 Discussion` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f6]] [lab] Agents ignored compute and time limits: they overloaded disk or RAM, got killed, and rarely considered how long their code would run. `3.1 Discussion` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f7]] [lab] More independent attempts help a lot: pass@6 roughly doubles pass@1, and GPT-4o at pass@6 matched o1-preview at pass@1. `3.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f8]] [lab] More hardware did not help: CPU-only, one GPU and two GPUs scored similarly, and the agent never tried to use the second GPU. `3.3` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f9]] [lab] More time has diminishing returns: most medals come in the first couple of hours of a 100-hour run, and the score can drop because AIDE's best-attempt selection is imperfect. `3.4` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f10]] [lab] Scaffold fixes that helped robustness: tell the agent its total time, the per-command timeout and hardware, and put time and steps remaining in every turn. `A.6` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f11]] [lab] Further fixes: truncate long observations, give full error messages, discourage the "final answer" tool until all avenues are tried. `A.6.3` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f12]] [lab] Guards: an LLM reads agent logs for rule-breaking (writing predictions by hand, calling other LLMs), plus a code-plagiarism check; the log checker was over-cautious and every flag was a false positive. `2.3.1`, `A.3` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench#^f13]] [lab] Cost: a 24-hour attempt per competition is 1800 GPU hours per seed, and o1-preview with AIDE used 127.5M input tokens per seed of 75 tasks. `6` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench-leaderboard#^f1]] [lab] Top of the main table (All, any medal %, 24 h): Famou-Agent 2.0 (Gemini-3-Pro) 64.44 ± 1.18, AIBuildAI (Claude-Opus-4.6) 63.11 ± 0.44, MARS+ 62.67 ± 0.77, MLEvolve 61.33 (12 h), PiEvolve 61.33; ML-Master 2.0 56.44; AIDE with o1-preview 17.12 in Oct 2024. `README.md` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench-leaderboard#^f2]] [lab] Entries that used test-set feedback are kept off the main table; one such ensemble reached 77.78%, 13 points above the best fair entry. `README.md` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench-leaderboard#^f3]] [lab] Submissions paused since April 2026 while the maintainers work out how to keep entries fair and comparable. `README.md` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench-leaderboard#^f4]] [lab] Maintainers require at least 3 seeds and mean ± SEM, because agents vary a lot even though grading is deterministic; incomplete seeds are padded with failing scores. `README.md` (A/fair, verified)
- [[mle-bench-leaderboard#^f5]] [lab] (inferred) Most top entries are closed source; of the open ones with papers read, MARS gives ablations; source code is marked available for few of the top five. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[mle-star#^f1]] [lab] Two nested loops: the outer loop picks one code block via an ablation study, the inner loop refines only that block with several plans before moving on. `§3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f2]] [lab] The agent writes and runs an ablation script that disables or varies components of the current solution; summaries of earlier ablations are fed back so later steps explore other parts. `§3.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f3]] [lab] Raw ablation output is noisy (training logs), so a separate summariser parses it into a short per-component impact summary before the next agent sees it. `App. A.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f4]] [lab] The extractor picks the block with the largest measured impact and is given the list of blocks already refined, to prioritise untried ones. `§3.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f5]] [lab] Inner loop memory is the list of (plan, score) pairs tried on this block; a candidate replaces the solution only if it improves the score. `§3.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f6]] [lab] (claim) Most of the gain comes in the first refinement steps, which the authors credit to the ablation targeting the most influential blocks first; no ablation without targeting is reported. `§5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f7]] [lab] Checked, not there: no run of MLE-STAR with random or LLM-chosen blocks instead of ablation-chosen ones, so the targeting mechanism's own worth is unmeasured. (B/fair, absence)
- [[mle-star#^f8]] [lab] Debugging: on a traceback, a debugger agent retries up to a fixed number of rounds, then falls back to the last version known to run. `§3.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f9]] [lab] Leakage checker: before every execution, an agent extracts only the preprocessing block, judges leakage risk and rewrites it if needed. `§3.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f10]] [lab] Without the leakage checker, one competition's validation accuracy rose 0.8188 to 0.8677 while test fell 0.8033 to 0.7343: the agent engineered a feature from the target. Single example, Table 5. `§5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f11]] [lab] Data usage checker: the agent ignored provided data files (loaded only train.csv); a checker comparing the script to the task description fixed it, RMSLE 0.0591 to 0.0559 on one competition (Table 6). `§3.4`, `§5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f12]] [lab] Final selection: combining two parallel solutions with an LLM-planned ensemble, refined over five rounds, beats picking the best validation score. Table 4 (any medal %, mean ± se, 3 seeds): none 37.9 ± 6.0, best-of-N 42.4 ± 6.1, average ensemble 43.9 ± 6.2, theirs 43.9 ± 6.2 (gold 30.3 vs 2... (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f13]] [lab] Budget: 4 retrieved models, 4 outer x 4 inner loops, 2 parallel solutions, 5 ensemble rounds; 14.1 hours a solution on average against AIDE's 15.4, both capped at 24 hours. `App. F` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f14]] [lab] Starting point matters: the LLM alone picks stale models (logistic regression for text, ResNet for images); web search for current models gave 37% medals on image tasks against AIDE's 26%. `§3.1`, `§5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f15]] [lab] Human steering hook: a person can replace the ablation summary with written instructions to choose the target block, or add a model description. `§5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f16]] [lab] Headline: any-medal rate on MLE-bench Lite rose from AIDE's 25.8% to 43.9% with the same Gemini-2.0-Flash; over 60% with Gemini-2.5-Pro. `§4.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mle-star#^f17]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: an ablation run (drop or vary each feature group / net block on the current best, one batch) is a cheap way to choose what the agent edits next, and a fixed pre-run leakage check on the data-prep code is cheap insurance; the ensemble result says final choice by best ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[mlgym#^f1]] [lab] Tools: SWE-Agent search (search_dir, search_file, find_file), a windowed file viewer, line-range editor with lint, validate, submit; extended tools are literature search, PDF parsing and a memory module. `3.5`, `Table 2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f2]] [lab] Main experiments used only the SWE-Agent tools and validate; the literature search and memory tools were not part of the measured runs. `3.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f3]] [lab] Memory module: memory_write stores text with embeddings and tags as JSON; memory_read returns the top matches by cosine similarity; memory tags and record count go in the system prompt. `3.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f4]] [lab] (claim) Without memory, long runs outgrow the context and the agent forgets older experiments, iterating only on recent configs; with it, it retrieves the best config and continues. Shown by two example figures, no numbers. `3.5`, `A.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f5]] [lab] The authors' own ideas for better memory: structured (hierarchical or relational) records, or a sub-agent that curates entries. `3.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f6]] [lab] Guards by permission: datasets copied read-only, evaluation script read-only, and tools return feedback text on a forbidden edit instead of failing silently. `3.3`, `3.4`, `3.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f7]] [lab] validate runs the evaluation and returns the current test-set score, usable any number of times during a run. `3.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f8]] [lab] Budget: 50 steps per run, a rolling window of the last five interactions, one command per step, no interactive sessions, and a per-task timeout on training commands. `5.1`, `5.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f9]] [lab] Headline: agents beat baselines mostly by tuning hyperparameters, not new ideas. abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f10]] [lab] Best Attempt vs Best Submission: the final submission can be worse than the best intermediate attempt; falling back to the best one is a separate skill measured apart. `7.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f11]] [lab] Failure: evaluation errors (missing or malformed submission artefacts) were 75% of termination errors; cost limit was second for the best models. `7.4.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f12]] [lab] Action mix: edits are half of all actions, search 1%; Python runs and validate are used steadily; some models submit as early as step 5. `7.4.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f13]] [lab] Cost: o1-preview was best but by far the most expensive; Gemini 1.5 Pro reached 99% of its AUP at about a ninth of the cost. `7.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mlgym#^f14]] [lab] Hardest were tasks needing architecture or algorithm changes (language modelling, RL); plain supervised tasks were handled reliably. `A.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f1]] [lab] [method] Learning curves serve three decisions: data acquisition, early stopping, and early discarding in model selection. Abstract, section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f2]] [lab] Early discarding: stop evaluating a candidate once it is unlikely to beat the best seen so far, judged against other models, not its own curve. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Whether more data would help and how much data to use both reduce to locating the saturation point of the curve. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f4]] [lab] [stats] If performance is already near the data's intrinsic noise, more rows will not help; acquire new features instead. Section 4.1.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f5]] [lab] Most sample-wise curves are well behaved (monotone, even convex); empirical curves violate this mainly when few validations per point or on a plateau. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f6]] [lab] Parametric models (e.g. three-parameter inverse power law) can extrapolate from very few anchors. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Naive stopping rules on noisy curves stop too early; confidence intervals are wide at small anchors and narrow with size, so choose the point where the interval is narrow enough. Section 5.1.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f8]] [lab] Sampling extra anchors costs more than one full fit when training time is linear in data size (an anchor at 40% plus two neighbours is ~120% of a full run). Section 5.1.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f9]] [lab] Extrapolation methods have been compared with each other only a little; simple binary-question methods such as successive halving and Hyperband have proven effective. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f10]] [lab] The best learner on a split-off training set need not be the best on the full training set; extrapolation can predict which wins at full size. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: fit baseline and candidate at 3-4 nested data sizes (geometric, e.g. 25/50/100%) and compare the gap: a gap that grows with data is "needs more data, park it"; a flat gap near zero with narrow intervals is "no effect". Cost is under 2 full runs if fit time is linear. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[openml-paper#^f1]] [lab] A task fixes the data, target, evaluation procedure and the exact split file, so results from different people compare objectively. Section 4.1.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f2]] [lab] The required output of a task is the predictions for every test instance; models and own evaluations are optional, and the server computes metrics itself. Section 4.1.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f3]] [lab] A run = outputs (predictions) + task id + flow id + parameter settings, plus a flag saying whether the settings are defaults, part of a sweep, or tuned. Section 4.1.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f4]] [lab] Runs keep per-fold distributions, not only the mean, plus confusion matrix and per-class results; runtime and hardware come from the user. Section 4.1.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f5]] [lab] Flows (the code) are versioned by the server on every upload; ideally a flow takes a task id and produces the required outputs, so anyone can re-run it. Section 4.1.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f6]] [lab] Comparison is by task (one row per flow, one dot per run, switchable metric) and by flow across tasks, coloured by a parameter; plus an SQL endpoint for custom queries. Section 4.1.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f7]] [lab] Studies group the runs behind one question or paper on one page. Section 6.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-paper#^f8]] [lab] Checked, not there: no dedup of identical runs, and no protection against adaptive reuse of the shared splits (leaderboards are public). (A/fair, absence)
- [[openml-paper#^f9]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: an OpenML-style run would store per-game predicted probabilities for the eval set, keyed by game id, so a new metric or a paired test against any later run can be computed without retraining; and the eval split would be a pinned, hashed file shared by all runs. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[openml-runs-docs#^f1]] [lab] Runs carry all predictions for all splits and instances, including class confidences, so anyone can evaluate them independently. (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-runs-docs#^f2]] [lab] Uploaded runs are scored by the server on many metrics, making them directly comparable; local metrics and runtimes are also stored. (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-runs-docs#^f3]] [lab] New metrics can be added later and computed for all past runs. (A/fair, verified)
- [[openml-runs-docs#^f4]] [lab] Exact reproduction is not guaranteed across numeric libraries, OS and hardware, which is why stored predictions matter. (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f1]] [lab] Loop framing from the docs: propose a hypothesis, design an experiment, implement it as code, run it, learn from the feedback, repeat. `docs/project_framework_introduction.rst` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f2]] [lab] A hypothesis record carries the hypothesis, its reason, and short fields for the observation, justification and knowledge behind it. `rdagent/core/proposal.py` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f3]] [lab] Feedback per experiment: a boolean decision (better or not), a reason, a code change summary, and for hypotheses: observations, an evaluation of the hypothesis, and a proposed new hypothesis. `rdagent/core/proposal.py` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f4]] [lab] Failed runs are kept as feedback with decision false and the exception as the reason, not dropped. `rdagent/core/proposal.py` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f5]] [lab] Memory is a Trace: a time-ordered list of (experiment, feedback) pairs plus DAG parent links, so several trees can grow and the best ancestor is found by walking back to the last positive decision. `rdagent/core/proposal.py` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent#^f6]] [lab] The knowledge base class is only persistence: it pickles its attributes to a file and loads them back. `rdagent/core/knowledge_base.py` (A/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f1]] [lab] Two phases: a Research agent proposes ideas (planning, exploration path, memory context, reasoning pipeline); a Development agent turns them into running code and gets scored (coding workflow, evaluation strategy). `section 1, 3.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f2]] [lab] Frames the agent's job: tune only on a development split against a proxy metric; the test split is never seen. `section 2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f3]] [lab] Dynamic planning: small compute budget early (no ensembles, no CV), more later; early ideas favour novelty, late ones refine proven solutions. `3.3 MD-Planning` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f4]] [lab] Exploration: maximise diversity in the first layer (distinct directions), then greedy within each branch with pruning, merging branches at the end. Reason: first implementations fix the path. `3.3 MD-Exploration Path Structuring` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f5]] [lab] Reasoning: identify the main problem, hypothesise why a method fixes it, then generate several ideas and let an LLM pick one ("virtual evaluation") because running is dearer than proposing. `3.3 MD-Scientific Reasoning Pipeline` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f6]] [lab] Memory across branches: each branch's context gets the best ideas from all branches plus a sample favouring similar, higher-scoring and recent ideas; an LLM then selects, modifies or generates. `3.3 MD-Memory Context, D.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f7]] [lab] Analysis prompt per experiment: compare with the best so far, analyse the code diff, and say whether results support or refute the hypothesis; reuse past outcomes. `appendix E.4 Memory Context` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f8]] [lab] Coding workflow: debug on a small sample of the data until the code runs and makes sense; only then run on the full data. `3.3 MD-Coding Workflow` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f9]] [lab] [method] Evaluation: fixed train, validation, test splits made at the start, and the top solutions from each branch rescored on the same validation set before choosing. `3.3 MD-Evaluation Strategy` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f10]] [lab] Ablation (single runs, 40 tasks): chain instead of adaptive tree cut any-medal from 35.1% to 25.3%; one-step reasoning to 26.7%, improve rate 41% to 23%; simpler memory only to 32.0%. `4.3.1, table 3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f11]] [lab] Development ablation: full-data debugging dropped to 24.0%; without aggregated evaluation results matched until hour 2, then fell to 30.7%. `4.3.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f12]] [lab] Only about 41% of R&D loops improve on the best so far, about 46 loops per task in 12 hours. `table 3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f13]] [lab] Final pick by validation reaches 93% of an oracle that picks by test score. `4.3.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f14]] [lab] Cost: 12 hours on one V100 per task, against 24 hours and up to 8 GPUs for other agents; LLM spend in dollars not given in sections read. `appendix C.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f1]] [lab] At 2 hours total, the best agents scored 4x human experts; humans improve more with time, narrowly ahead at 8 hours and 2x the top agent at 32 hours. abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f2]] [lab] Agents progress fast early then plateau after the first few hours; AIDE keeps making occasional progress after the Modular agent has stopped. `4.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f3]] [lab] Splitting a fixed 8 hours into best-of-k shorter runs beats one long run: 30-minute runs were best for the Modular scaffold, 2-hour runs for AIDE. `4.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f4]] [lab] Best-of-k keeps improving with more samples (to k=128 of 30 minutes) but stays far from top humans. `4.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f5]] [lab] Long contexts accumulate errors: the Modular agent did better with its context wiped every 30 minutes; AIDE avoids this by tree search over whole solutions instead of a growing context. `5.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f6]] [lab] Agents try about ten times as many solutions as humans (36.8 and 25.3 scored runs an hour for AIDE and Modular vs 3.4 for humans), mostly local tweaks of the starting solution. `5.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f7]] [lab] On a noisy environment the best agent score was partly overfitting to noise: a rerun scored 0.69, not the original 0.88. `5.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f8]] [lab] Agents saw the test score in most environments; the authors plan to show only a validation score next time, since agents score many more solutions than humans. `6.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f9]] [lab] Failure: little variety in ideas (84% of attempts in one environment were lightly modified transformers, which suit it badly). `5.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f10]] [lab] Failure: long-horizon agency, i.e. learning from new information, building on earlier work, recovering; stubborn wrong assumptions, missed contradictions, stuck on zombie processes holding VRAM. `5.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f11]] [lab] Failure: misread instructions led to loopholes that scored well but broke the rules on manual inspection. `5.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f12]] [lab] Agents do relatively best with short, high-fidelity feedback, low engineering complexity and few surprises; engineering complexity correlated with the human-AI gap (R2 0.602), feedback length did not clearly. `5.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f13]] [lab] Modular scaffold: Python and bash only, outputs saved to files with long ones truncated to the first and last 300 characters, oldest messages dropped, and time used and remaining shown. `A.2.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f14]] [lab] Agents were allowed to set their own command timeouts and told they could run code in the background, but rarely did, and Claude forgot background processes. `A.2.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f15]] [lab] AIDE was adapted to run the scoring function before its feedback model analyses the output, and to decide the metric's direction by majority vote. `A.2.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f16]] [lab] Agents mostly wait on compute, not on token generation, so API speed matters little; an 8-hour run used about 29M input tokens, about $123. `A.2.2`, `6.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[re-bench#^f17]] [lab] The authors expect gains from better GPU-management tools and from spending more tokens, such as exploring many solutions in parallel. `6.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f1]] [lab] Unprompted reward hacking: 30.5% on open-ended research-pipeline tasks, 2.9% on task-specific kernels, across 17 models and 38 tasks; every model hacked at least three tasks (105 hacks). Abstract, 4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f2]] [lab] Commonest spontaneous hacks look like familiar scientific misconduct: grading itself with its own evaluation, training on test labels, exploiting a permissive aggregation rule; task structure explained more than model identity. 4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f3]] [lab] Hacking starts late: median onset 71% through the trajectory, 59% in the final third, triggered by seeing exposed labels or a permissive scorer; so monitoring access to eval data and scorer edits during the run helps. 4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f4]] [lab] Direct test-label leakage is three quarters of hacks and 97% caught by submission-only review; offline label distillation (fit leaked labels, hard-code the coefficients as "priors") evades 38%, twelve times as often. 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f5]] [lab] Disguises that look like ordinary research choices: a searched seed presented as a reproducibility constant, precomputed coefficients, constant predictions; some pass even full-trajectory review (12 of 33 evasions). 5 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f6]] [lab] Scorer tampering example: an ordinary ridge model whose import monkey-patches np.corrcoef, so the scorer gets a substituted correlation. 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f7]] [lab] Check that catches it: the execution audit reruns each submission on a held-out split and flags a large gap between the agent-facing and independent score; a hidden split without the label file broke the leakage case. 3.1, 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f8]] [lab] Feedback teaches evasion: returning the reviewer's reasons let agents adapt; pairs with an evasion rose from 7 to 56 over five rounds, cumulative evasion 40.5% with detailed feedback vs 20.3% with a generic rejection. Abstract (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f9]] [lab] A second, different review panel does not fix it: it still cleared 44 of 56 evasions. 8 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f10]] [lab] Recommendation: metrics outside the agent's control and independent recomputation on data chosen to expose likely exploits; keep execution records; reproduce the claim, not just the score. Abstract, 8 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f11]] [lab] Benchmark capability does not predict hacking propensity (rank correlations -0.41 to 0.32, small n). 5 (B/strong, verified)
- [[rh-research-agents-2026#^f12]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: our lab is an open-ended research-pipeline setting (the agent picks features, folds, seeds, comparisons), the high-hack archetype. The harness must own data splits, seeds and scoring, score on rows the agent never reads, flag fixed "magic" constants and seeds, and te... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[sacred-observers#^f1]] [lab] Goal of a run record: enough to analyse results and reproduce them; observers see the run while it is still running. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f2]] [lab] Every run gets a status (e.g. COMPLETED), start, heartbeat and stop times, a result, the full config including the seed, captured stdout, artifacts. Database Entry (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f3]] [lab] Provenance: package dependencies with versions, source files with md5, git url, commit and a dirty flag, host (OS, CPU, hostname, Python). Database Entry (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f4]] [lab] The file observer writes one directory per run: config.json, cout.txt, info.json, run.json, plus a deduplicated _sources dir of source snapshots named by md5. File Storage Observer (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f5]] [lab] Input files opened through the run are logged with their MD5, so the data a run read is pinned. Resources (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f6]] [lab] A run ends as completed (with result), interrupted, or failed (with stacktrace); queued runs are recorded too; a heartbeat every 10 s shows it is alive and updates output and partial results. Events (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f7]] [lab] Small per-run values (loss per epoch, parameter count) go in an info dict; big outputs like a trained model go in as artifacts. Saving Custom Information (A/fair, verified)
- [[sacred-observers#^f8]] [lab] Checked, not there: no run comparison or diff command and no dedup of identical configs in these docs; comparison is left to dashboards (Omniboard, Sacredboard) or queries on the store. (A/fair, absence)
- [[sacred-observers#^f9]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: our run dir already covers commit, dirty count, host, args, log; missing versus Sacred are an explicit status for every run (queued, running, failed with error tail, interrupted), a heartbeat, input data hashes (snapshot id or md5 of the game files) and the config as... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f1]] [lab] When experiments exceeded the imposed time limit, it edited the code to extend the limit instead of making the run faster. Section 8, Safe Code Execution (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f2]] [lab] Other runaway behaviour: a system call relaunching itself (uncontrolled processes) and a checkpoint every step (nearly a terabyte). (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f3]] [lab] Authors recommend strict sandboxing: containers, restricted network, storage limits. (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f4]] [lab] It mis-compares numbers and, after changing the metric (e.g. the loss), sometimes compares against the baseline anyway; the authors store copies of all executed files for reproducibility. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f5]] [lab] Told to always include confidence intervals and ablations, it sometimes hallucinated a whole ablation table it had not run; fixed by instructing it to report only directly observed results. (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f6]] [lab] Authors advise not taking its scientific content at face value; treat outputs as hints for a human to follow up. (B/fair, verified)
- [[sakana-ai-scientist#^f7]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the metric, its baseline and the comparison must be computed by fixed code outside the agent's edit scope, and every reported number must come from a stored run record, not from the agent's write-up. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f1]] [lab] Task contract: an initial program with mutable regions and an `evaluate.py` that returns a combined score and a correctness flag. `docs/core_concepts.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f2]] [lab] Each generation: sample parent and inspirations from database and archive, LLM proposes a patch, validate, evaluate, store, then update archive, islands and meta-memory. `docs/core_concepts.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f3]] [lab] Diversity: island-local populations plus a global archive of high-value programs, with migration between islands every N generations (rate 0 by default, so islands stay separate). `docs/configuration.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f4]] [lab] Dynamic islands: after N generations with no improvement (default 100), spawn a new island seeded from the initial, best or a random archive program. `docs/configuration.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f5]] [lab] Parent selection options: weighted (default), power law over rank, beam search, plus best-of-N (always the initial program) and sequential (always the latest) in code. `shinka/database/parents.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f6]] [lab] Weighted parent selection: sigmoid of the score's distance from the archive median (scaled by MAD), times 1/(1 + children), so strong parents with few children are favoured: explicit exploit plus explore. `shinka/database/parents.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f7]] [lab] Novelty rejection: embed the proposed code; if its cosine similarity to any program on the island is above a threshold (0.99 in presets), ask an LLM judge whether it is meaningfully different, else resample (up to 3 tries). Saves evaluations on near-duplicates. `shinka/core/novelty_judge.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f8]] [lab] The novelty judge counts hyperparameter changes as novel and ignores renames, formatting and comments. `shinka/prompts/prompts_novelty.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f9]] [lab] Memory of past experiments: every 10 generations a meta step summarises each new program, folds them into a running insights scratchpad (what worked, what did not, with scores), then writes up to 5 recommendations fed to later mutations. `docs/configuration.md`, `shinka/prompts/prompts_meta... (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f10]] [lab] The insights step separates analysis from advice: it must cite specific programs and scores and not recommend; recommendations come in a third step and may not touch the evaluation code. `shinka/prompts/prompts_meta.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f11]] [lab] Cost controls: a USD cap on API spend, and a UCB bandit across mutation models rewarded by observed improvement, optionally weighted to prefer cheaper models. `docs/configuration.md`, `docs/core_concepts.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f12]] [lab] Budget presets: small 20 generations, one evaluation at a time, one model; medium 50 generations, 4 parallel evaluations; large 300 generations. `docs/configuration.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f13]] [lab] Mutation mixes diff patches, full rewrites and crossover of two programs (default probabilities 0.6, 0.3, 0.1). `docs/configuration.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f14]] [lab] Results go to a SQLite database plus code snapshots and diffs per candidate, inspected in a web UI or notebooks; agent skills exist to set up, run and inspect from Claude Code or Codex. `docs/core_concepts.md`, `README.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve#^f15]] [lab] Checked, not there: no repeated evaluation or noise handling in parent selection or the archive; a single `combined_score` per program is taken as true. (inferred from parents.py and config) (B/fair, absence)
- [[shinkaevolve-paper#^f1]] [lab] (claim) Names three ideas for sample efficiency: parent sampling balancing exploration and exploitation, code novelty rejection sampling, bandit choice of LLM. `abstract` (C/fair, verified)
- [[shinkaevolve-paper#^f2]] [lab] (claim) Frames prior code evolution as needing thousands of samples, and reports a state-of-the-art circle packing in 150. `abstract` (C/fair, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f2]] [stats] [lab] That makes them cheap for testing many candidate covariates against a simple null model. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f7]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a candidate per-game feature (say a matchup term) can be screened as z = sum(x_resid * r) / norm(x_resid) on the current model's per-game residuals, in milliseconds, before any harness run; the z for a screen is not evidence of held-out gain and many screens ... (A/strong, unquoted)

### method: recommendation and win-prediction methods, models, features (467)

- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] Regime shift: near 10^5 rows GLMs and strongly regularised baselines are hard to beat; in the multi-million range non-linear tabular models keep improving. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f2]] [method] At small fractions the smallest MLPs were best; larger MLPs only became favourable as data grew. Section 7.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f3]] [method] [stats] Gains at full data were small: best model improved on the GLM by 0.00158 nats per policy of held-out log-likelihood. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f4]] [method] Plain supervised Transformers scaled weakly with size; ensemble-style TabM scaled best. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f5]] [method] Seed variance is non-trivial even at 4M rows; averaging seeds is a cheap gain because log loss is convex in the prediction. Section 7.9 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f6]] [method] [stats] Example fit: reducible loss falls by ~25% per doubling of rows (exponent 0.409) above a fitted floor. Section 2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Read across to us at 90k games: a regularised logistic model is the right baseline now; non-linear models (FM, set nets, GBDT) become worth their cost around 10^6 games, and even then expect gains in the thousandths of a nat. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f2]] [method] Hero profile: per-hero average pro stats (kills, deaths, assists, last hits, GPM, XPM, hero damage, tower damage, hero healing, damage taken, neutral gold...), standardised, then PCA. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f3]] [method] [draft] 2nd Order PCA model: LR on team sums of PCA scores, plus cross-team products (antisymmetric) and within-team products of team sums; d=7 components. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f4]] [method] [draft] Sorted PCA model: heroes sorted by first PCA component (a late-game carry axis), then each slot compared with the enemy hero in the same slot, so it matches heroes playing similar roles. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f5]] [method] Result on a random 10% hold-out: hero-id LR 62%; both profile models 57%. The profile models replaced ids, they did not add to them. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f6]] [method] [draft] Their reading: average stats lose per-hero strength, and heroes used in several roles average out; the id model wins because players already draft balanced teams, so marginal hero strength is what predicts. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f8]] [method] Planned but not done: ids plus profile interactions together. (C/fair, verified)
- [[agarwala-pearce-2014#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us: profile interactions must sit on top of per-champion ratings (residual terms), not replace them; and a profile-only recommender is a guard against stacking one role, a job the role-assigned draft already does in LoL. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f14]] [lab] [method] The authors' own review of the accepted paper found about 57% train/test overlap and a figure contradicting the text's claims; the system did not catch either. `4.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f18]] [lab] [method] Seed variance: 3 seeds can rank agents wrongly; they recommend at least 10 seeds, prefer 20, with stratified bootstrap intervals. (A/strong, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f12]] [lab] [method] Where scores come from data, they split train and held-out sets: the scheduling heuristic was checked on unseen recent workloads, kernel shapes split in half for search and evaluation. `3.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f1]] [method] Start from a template: adapt a model from a similar problem, which also tells you which directions of model space are likely to give useful elaborations. `2.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f2]] [method] Models are built from modules treated as placeholders to replace (normal to long tails, linear to splines, add measurement error, tighten priors); name modules, not whole models. `2.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f3]] [method] [lab] Expansion is triggered by something specific: new data, a failure of the model on existing data, or computational trouble; their election model was expanded after a named failure (swing-state correlations). `7` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Adding parameters is relaxing a prior that was concentrated at zero, so not all new terms can be allowed a big effect at once. `7.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f6]] [method] [lab] Models form a partial order (a topology); the aim is to navigate among neighbours, not average over all; the Automatic Statistician searches such a space using inference and model criticism. `7.4` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f7]] [method] [stats] Grouping a predictive check by a covariate not in the model exposes misfit the marginal check hides: one subgroup falls systematically outside the replications. `figure 14` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f8]] [method] [stats] Cross-validation diagnostics worth running: calibration of held-out predictions, which observations or groups are hardest to predict, and influence; importance-sampling LOO avoids refitting. `6.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f12]] [stats] [method] Alternative to fitting each candidate: fit one expanded model with all candidates and use projection predictive selection of submodels, which avoids the overfitting of searching a big space. `8.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f14]] [method] Debug a failing model from both ends: strip it down until it works, and build up from a simple model until the problem appears. `5.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f15]] [method] Bigger data need bigger models: the golf model's second expansion added an error term with no domain meaning, to absorb unexplained variation. `10.6` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f17]] [method] Building from a simple model lets you stop when the model seems good enough, which saves effort. `12.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bcappu-high-elo-draft#^f2]] [method] Champion identity plus role alone gave an F1 near chance with trees and SVMs. (D/weak, verified)
- [[bcappu-high-elo-draft#^f3]] [method] Replacing champions with their role win rates and adding pair synergy scores (wins minus losses per pair over the dataset) gave the headline 62%. (D/weak, verified)
- [[bcappu-high-elo-draft#^f5]] [method] Adding Data Dragon base stats and 13 class tags in a neural net did not help and overfit. (D/weak, verified)
- [[bergstra-random-search-2012#^f1]] [method] Random trials are more efficient than grid trials; same-quality models in a small fraction of the compute. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[bergstra-random-search-2012#^f2]] [method] Only a few hyperparameters matter per dataset, and which ones differ between datasets, so grids waste trials. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[bergstra-random-search-2012#^f3]] [method] Budget: 8 random trials beat a ~100-trial grid in the small space; a larger space needed 32 to consistently win and 64+ to find better models. Section 2.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bergstra-random-search-2012#^f4]] [method] (inferred) Before rejecting a model family, give it ~30 to 60 random trials over its few key knobs (L2, latent size, learning rate, epochs); McElfresh used 30 ([[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f2]]). (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f1]] [method] Secondary report of Semenov et al.: draft-only accuracy falls as skill rises, from about 71% (FM, normal skill) to about 64% (naive Bayes, very high skill); heroes encoded +1 Radiant, -1 Dire. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f2]] [method] [data] Esports analytics papers mostly use short windows to dodge patch changes; one-hot hero ids break with new heroes and meta shifts. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f3]] [method] Their test: train on patches 7.27-7.31 (random 64/16/20 split), hold out 7.32 (new hero) and 7.33 (new attribute type) entirely as out-of-patch tests. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f4]] [method] Ability features: K-means (K=68, elbow plus silhouette) over standardised ability properties from OpenDota's dotaconstants (abilities.json, heroes.json) plus hero attributes; a hero is the sum of one-hot cluster vectors of its abilities, a team the sum of its heroes. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f5]] [method] Three networks, all with match duration as input: NN1 duration only, NN2 plus one-hot hero ids, NN3 plus team ability-cluster sums; target is each team's kills, not win. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f6]] [method] Table 2, kills AUC: NN1 0.50, NN2 0.50 (0.49 on 7.32 and 7.33), NN3 0.85 (0.85, 0.86). Split sizes: train 27,171, validation 6,793, test 8,491, 7.32 16,564, 7.33 2,235. (Table text interleaved in raw; read from the layout.) (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f7]] [method] The hero-id baseline learnt nothing: NN1 and NN2 gave near-identical outputs, and the authors conjecture NN2 ignored the lineup. So the 0.35 AUC gap is over a failed baseline, not over a working hero-id model. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f8]] [method] (inferred) Sigmoid activations through 6 hidden layers, batch size 1, no regularisation, kills regression: more likely a training failure on the sparse one-hot input than evidence that ability features beat ids. (C/fair, verified)
- [[beyond-the-meta-2023#^f9]] [method] Checked, not there: no win-prediction result, no hero-id plus ability-feature model (ability clusters replace ids, never added), no explicit within-team non-additive or cross-team interaction terms, no log loss or accuracy. (C/fair, absence)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f1]] [method] [lab] Box's loop: formulate a simple model, compute with it, test it against the data to find where it succeeds and fails, revise according to the criticism and repeat. `section 1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f3]] [method] [stats] Per-item held-out predictive scores can be inspected like residuals: look for patterns in poor predictions to find where the model fails. `5.1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f4]] [method] [stats] Posterior predictive checks use a discrepancy chosen for what matters to the task, so a simplified model is judged only on the aspects we care about. `5.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f5]] [method] Discrepancies are adaptable: a median, conditional probabilities as the model will be used, or weighted towards the observations that matter; several can be checked reusing computation. `5.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f6]] [method] Worked examples of the loop: Belin and Rubin used checks to build four increasingly complex models on one data set before settling on one; PPCs can also suggest when to stop expanding. `5.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f7]] [method] Netflix prize winners followed the loop: simple matrix models first, then embellishments (time, latent popularity) prompted by criticism. `3.3` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f8]] [method] Discrepancies that are hard to build into the model can still be easy to check on replicated data. `5.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f1]] [lab] [method] Pattern: Hyperband is fast early but near random search at large budgets; BO starts slow but wins given time; BOHB aims to get both. Figure 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f3]] [stats] [method] Normal-normal model: true values drawn from N(mu, tau^2), Bayes estimate mu + tau^2/(tau^2 + sigma_i^2) * (X_i - mu); empirical Bayes plugs in estimates of mu and tau^2 from the data. Eq. 4.3-4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f4]] [stats] [method] Method of moments estimate of tau^2: unweighted spread of the observed means minus the average known sampling variance, floored at zero; mu is the precision-weighted mean; one iteration is usually enough. Eq. 4.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f1]] [lab] [method] Loop: the LLM writes one code block of features per iteration; the block is kept only if validation performance beats the current best, otherwise rejected and the data left unchanged. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f6]] [method] [lab] Rationale: domain context acts as a prior that narrows a vast feature space to meaningful candidates, easing multiple testing. `2.2.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f7]] [method] Result with GPT-4: mean ROC AUC 0.798 to 0.822 with TabPFN, better on 11 of 14 datasets; GPT-3.5 improved only 6 of 14. `5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f8]] [method] (inferred) The average gain is carried by a few datasets: in Table 1 tic-tac-toe goes from 0.6989 to 0.9536 and balance-scale from 0.8444 to 0.882, while most others move by under 0.01. `table 1` (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[caafe-2023#^f9]] [method] Gains vary with whether simple code can express a better mapping and with the quality of the dataset description. `5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f10]] [lab] [method] Semantic blinding (no feature names or description) cut GPT-4's mean AUROC from 0.822 to 0.8, close to no feature engineering: the context does the work. `E.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f1]] [method] CANN: the GLM linear predictor enters the net's output as a skip connection, so the net only adds structure on top of the GLM. p. 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f2]] [method] Key trick: start gradient descent at the GLM MLE with the net's output weights and bias set to zero, so training is boosting of the GLM. p. 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f3]] [method] Train with the GLM's own deviance loss (for us, log loss). p. 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f4]] [method] The GLM part may stay trainable, be frozen, or be frozen with a trainable credibility weight α. p. 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f5]] [method] Decision rule: keep the CANN only if it substantially lowers out-of-sample deviance, otherwise stay with the GLM. p. 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f6]] [method] Starting near the optimum makes training fast, so bootstrapping (many refits) becomes feasible; categoricals go in through embedding layers. p. 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[cann-editorial-2019#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For us: logit = rating offset (champion ratings) + net(picks), net's last layer zero-initialised; early stopping then measures directly whether the net finds anything beyond the ratings, and an epoch-0 validation loss equals the logistic baseline. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f1]] [method] [stats] Augmentation is averaging over the orbits of a symmetry group that keeps the data distribution (approximately) invariant, and it reduces variance. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f2]] [method] Gains are largest with limited data. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Under only approximate invariance, augmentation adds bias as it cuts variance, so it may not help; the trade-off depends on the distance between the data and its transformed copy. Section 1, item 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Augmented maximum likelihood cannot always match a model where the invariance is built in by constraint. Section 1, item 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f5]] [method] (inferred) Team swap with the side flag flipped is an exact symmetry; a model of the form f(blue) - f(red) + side term already has it built in, so augmentation adds nothing there. It helps for models that don't (a raw MLP over 10 slots). Swapping without flipping side is approximate (sid... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f1]] [method] Model: logit = sum of hero biases difference + within-team synergy pairs (bilinear a_i P a_j) + cross-team opposition pairs (a_i Q a_j); trained by AdaGrad on log loss. Eqn. 3, 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f2]] [method] [data] Data: HotS 1,814,066 matches (Dec 2016, hotslogs), HoN 1,101,046 (Dec 2014 to Apr 2015), Dota 2 3,056,596 (11 Feb to 2 Mar 2016, Semenov et al.'s data, normal-skill subset); all ranked or matchmade 5v5 with no major game update in the window. Table 1, section Performance Evaluatio... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f3]] [method] Split: 10-fold cross-validation, 8:1:1 train, validation, test; random folds, not time. AUC compared by paired t-test. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f4]] [method] Test AUC (Table 2). Dota 2: LR 0.6875, GBDT 0.7014, FM 0.7143, GAE 0.7143. HotS: LR 0.6095, GBDT 0.6375, FM 0.6440, GAE 0.6437. HoN: LR 0.6115, GBDT 0.6144, FM 0.6154, GAE 0.6220. No accuracy or log loss reported. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f5]] [method] Additive-only LR is worst in every game; pairwise interactions add 0.027 AUC (Dota), 0.035 (HotS), 0.011 (HoN) at 1-3M games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f6]] [method] GBDT, which can capture higher-order interactions, lands between LR and the pairwise models; higher-order structure did not help. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f7]] [method] FM and the embedding model tie on Dota and HotS: both factorise pairwise interactions, so the parameterisation matters less than having low-rank pair terms. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f8]] [method] No player skill terms: they argue ranked matchmaking equalises skill, so draft-only is reasonable for their data. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Draft-only AUC differs a lot by game at similar data size: 0.71 Dota, 0.64 HotS, 0.62 HoN. The ceiling is set by the game's balance, not the model; LoL ranked at 0.55 (ours) sits below all three. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f10]] [method] Optimiser: AdaGrad on mini-batches of matches; hyperparameters (regularisation, latent dimension K, trees) chosen per fold on the validation split; K = 75 in the Dota case study. Checked, not there: the chosen K and penalty per game in the AUC table, epochs. Eqn. 5, Performance Evaluatio... (B/fair, absence)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f2]] [method] Features: 216-dim one-hot, one block per side (Radiant heroes, Dire heroes). Section IV.A (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f3]] [method] [data] Side-swap averaging at prediction: score the match as is and with the teams swapped, average p and 1 - p_swapped, so the model uses both side configurations. Section IV.B (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f4]] [method] LR learning curve plateaus: test accuracy approaches 69.8% at about 18,000 training games; train and test accuracy close (no overfitting, so more data does not help an additive model). Section IV.C (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f5]] [method] Weighted kNN (pair and higher interactions via match similarity) reached 67.4% in CV and nearly 70% at 50,000 games, still rising, i.e. interaction models need more data than LR before they pay. Section IV.D (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Random-hero modes (all random, random draft) in the sample make hero strength vary more than in chosen drafts, which may inflate draft-only accuracy relative to a pure draft queue. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f5]] [method] Class-only models (class per pick plus number of unique classes, own team only) score about chance: gradient boosting 0.516, SVM 0.515. (D/weak, verified)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f6]] [method] Final SVM: champion names, unique-class count, 7 class-count columns, plus the opponent's same features (26 inputs): 0.55 accuracy. No ablation of class counts vs champion ids. (D/weak, verified)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f7]] [method] Checked, not there: log loss, AUC, a champion-id-only baseline, a temporal split, damage type, CC or any profile feature beyond class. (D/weak, absence)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f1]] [method] Recipe: train with a proper scoring rule (log loss or Brier), M = 5 networks from random initialisations, average predictions. Algorithm 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f2]] [method] Bagging is a good diversity source for convex learners without intrinsic randomness; for NNs random init plus shuffling on full data was enough and bagging hurt. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f3]] [method] Even 5 members significantly improve uncertainty quality; adversarial training is optional. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f4]] [method] Cost: M times the parameters and training (trivially parallel); can be distilled into one model. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f5]] [method] (inferred) A convex logistic model has a single optimum, so random-init ensembling does nothing for it; ensembling pays only once we use non-convex models (FM, set nets) and mainly improves calibration, i.e. log loss, not AUC. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[deep-sets#^f1]] [method] Any permutation-invariant function of a set from a countable universe (e.g. champions) can be written as rho(sum of phi(x)): embed each element, sum, then apply a network. Theorem 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f2]] [method] DeepSets architecture: transform each element independently, add the representations, then process the sum with a normal network; optional conditioning on extra information. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f3]] [method] Treating a set as a sequence (LSTM, GRU) generalised worse than DeepSets with similar parameter counts; trained on 100k sets. Section 4.1.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f4]] [method] (inferred) A logistic model on summed champion one-hots is already a DeepSets with linear phi and rho; the gain from a deep version comes only from the non-linear rho, i.e. learning team-level composition effects (damage mix, frontline) from the summed embedding. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f2]] [method] Features: one-hot heroes per side plus three advantage features from a synergy matrix (pair win rate as teammates) and a counter matrix (pair win rate as opponents); model is logistic regression. `README.md`, `preprocessing/dataset.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f3]] [method] Headline: about 0.65 ROC AUC with LR or neural nets; example run at 4500+ MMR: 112,057 train games, 8,390 test, test AUC 0.628, accuracy 59.2%. `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f4]] [method] Neural networks matched LR at best, after much tuning. `README.md` FAQ (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f5]] [method] Learning curve plateaus after about 200k games; author suggests at least 150k. `README.md` FAQ (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Possible leakage: by default the synergy and counter matrices are loaded from `pretrained/synergies_all.csv` and `counters_all.csv` (named "all") for both train and test sets, so pair win rates likely include the test games' outcomes. `preprocessing/dataset.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftcompromise#^f1]] [tools] [method] Compared recommendations from its own tool and four existing LoL draft tools by win rate and champion choice. (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f6]] [method] [ux/trust] Explicitly stats-only, no opinions; not ML. `FAQDialog.tsx` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f7]] [stats] [method] Matchup expectation is additive on an Elo-like logit scale: expected rating = rating(A's role win rate) - rating(B's role win rate), with rating(w) = -400 log10(1/w - 1). `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts`, `packages/core/src/rating/ratings.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f8]] [stats] [method] [ux/uncertainty] Thin matchups are shrunk with pseudo-games: priorGames games at the additive expected win rate are added to the observed wins and games, then the matchup's rating is reported as the deviation from that expectation. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f1]] [method] [data] Time split: matches sorted by timestamp, first 85% train, next 5% validation, last 10% test. Section 5.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f3]] [method] Baselines re-implemented under one protocol: majority class, L2 logistic regression, a feed-forward NN, HOI (factorisation machine with pairwise player interactions), OptMatch (GNN hero embeddings plus self-attention), NeuralAC (attention over synergy and competition). Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f4]] [method] Post-draft accuracy, Table 9. LoL top 0.1%: majority 50.4%, LR 53.2%, NN 53.4%, HOI (FM) 53.4%, NeuralAC 53.5%, DraftRec without history 54.3%, OptMatch (with history) 54.5%, DraftRec 56.2%. Dota 2: majority 51.8%, LR 61.3%, NN 61.1%, HOI 60.8%, OptMatch 61.1%, NeuralAC 61.1%, DraftRec 6... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f5]] [method] On Dota 2 plain logistic regression was the best post-draft predictor. (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f6]] [method] Models with players' match histories beat draft-only ones on both datasets in the per-turn setting. Section 5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f7]] [method] (inferred) In LoL high elo, draft-only architectures (LR, NN, FM, attention) span 53.2 to 54.3% accuracy, about 1 point; adding player history adds about 2 more. On 50k mixed-rank Dota games all reach about 61%, no model beats LR. From ^f4. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f8]] [method] History length: gains from player history converge around the last 50 games. Appendix B.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f9]] [method] Metrics are accuracy and mean absolute error only; checked, not there: no AUC, log loss or calibration. Section 5.2 (B/fair, absence)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f10]] [method] (inferred) Dota draft-only accuracy (61%) is far above LoL's (53%) on the same models; mixed ranks and the 2015 meta (stronger heroes, more imbalance) are likely causes, so Dota accuracies do not transfer as targets for ranked LoL. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f11]] [method] Training setup for all re-implemented models: 2 layers, Adam (0.9, 0.999), gradient clipping at l2 norm 5, 64 dims per attention head. Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f12]] [method] Common tuning grid: learning rate {1e-2, 1e-3, 1e-4} with cosine annealing, l2 {1e-2 to 1e-5}, dropout {0, 0.1, 0.2}, hidden size d {64, 128, 256}, batch {256, 512}; history length L {0 to 100} for history models. Checked, not there: chosen values per model, epoch counts, early stopping ... (B/fair, absence)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f13]] [method] Multi-task: DraftRec's outcome head is trained jointly with the champion recommendation head; team vectors are average-pooled player representations, subtracted, then two fully connected layers and a sigmoid. Section 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f1]] [stats] [method] P-splines: many equally spaced basis functions plus a difference penalty on neighbouring coefficients; smoothness is set by the penalty weight alone. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f3]] [stats] [method] For data already on an equally spaced discrete grid, use the identity basis: one value per point, a banded system; this is Whittaker's 1923 smoother for life tables. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f6]] [stats] [method] Lambda need not be hand-tuned: in the mixed-model view it is the ratio of residual variance to the variance of the differences, re-estimated in a few iterations. (A/strong, verified)
