- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Repeated leaderboard evaluation lets participants overfit the holdout; Kaggle's existing defences are heuristics (bit precision, resubmission rate). Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Kaggle's public/private split protects the final ranking but not the public scores, which forums report as inflated. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Ladder: only release a new score if it beats the previous best by a margin; otherwise repeat the previous best. Section 3, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Error of naive leaderboards grows like sqrt(k) in the number of submissions; the Ladder's grows only with log(k), an exponential improvement. Section 1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f5]] [lab] [stats] Parameter-free variant: the margin is a one-sided paired t-test on the per-example loss vectors of the new and previous best submission (threshold s/sqrt(n)), since paired tests have more power when losses are correlated. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f6]] [lab] [stats] The paper says its significance test is heuristic: adaptivity breaks the t approximation and multiple comparisons are not controlled. Section 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Boosting attack: submitting random label vectors and majority-voting those that scored better makes a submission biased on the public labels only; on the hidden 8000 labels it stays at chance, while Kaggle's mechanism reports a strongly biased loss. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Under that attack the Ladder stays within the expected maximum deviation of k random vectors: each new best makes the next crossing less likely, so only O(log k) steps. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f9]] [lab] [stats] An analyst who only uses the holdout to check whether the latest attempt is well above the previous best effectively runs the Ladder. Conclusion (A/strong, verified)
- [[bohb#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Where low budgets are too noisy to predict the full budget, cheap evaluations just add overhead and plain BO wins (64-dimension case). Section 5.1, appendix H (A/fair, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Recommendations: randomise as many sources of variation as possible; decide on improvement with variance, via the probability A beats B in a run; prefer resampling (out-of-bootstrap) over one fixed held-out test set for small gains. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Data sampling (bootstrap of the data) is the largest source of variance; weight initialisation is usually under half of it, on par with data order. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f3]] [stats] Test-set noise in accuracy matches a binomial with n test samples; with correlated errors the distribution is wider. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The total variance from arbitrary choices is on the order of published state-of-the-art increments. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f6]] [lab] [stats] Fixing seeds conditions on arbitrary values and correlates runs, so more splits stop reducing the estimator's variance; randomising seeds and data together decorrelates them. Section 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Re-seeding only the init (common practice) barely helps beyond k = 1; randomising data splits too, and all non-HPO sources, is far better and 51x cheaper than redoing HPO each time (21 vs 1070 hours at k = 100). Section 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Decision error rates (simulated, k = 50): a single-run comparison has about 10% false positives and 75% false negatives; a mean difference with a published-improvement threshold has under 5% false positives but about 90% false negatives; P(A>B) gives about 5% and 30%. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f9]] [lab] [stats] Rule: A beats B if P(A>B) over paired splits is significantly above 0.5 (bootstrap CI) and meaningfully reaches gamma, with gamma = 0.75 robust across their cases. Sections 4.1, 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f15]] [lab] [stats] Monitor 7: calibration by slice as a canary (90% of 0.9 predictions should be positive); unbiased is not good, but bias flags a problem. Section V (A/fair, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f1]] [stats] Using each unit's raw average as its prediction (the naive method) is worse than ignoring individual data and predicting the overall mean. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f2]] [stats] Shrinkage scales with sample size: units with fewer observations are pulled harder toward the group mean; the best estimator cut squared error by about 40% relative to the overall mean. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f3]] [stats] [method] Normal-normal model: true values drawn from N(mu, tau^2), Bayes estimate mu + tau^2/(tau^2 + sigma_i^2) * (X_i - mu); empirical Bayes plugs in estimates of mu and tau^2 from the data. Eq. 4.3-4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f4]] [stats] [method] Method of moments estimate of tau^2: unweighted spread of the observed means minus the average known sampling variance, floored at zero; mu is the precision-weighted mean; one iteration is usually enough. Eq. 4.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f5]] [stats] Out-of-sample ranking (relative squared error, naive = 1): nonparametric EB 0.508, James-Stein 0.525, EB method of moments 0.593, group mean 0.852, harmonic prior 0.884, EB maximum likelihood 0.902. Table 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f6]] [stats] Why ML failed: sample size correlated with the true value (better hitters bat more), so the n-weighted ML estimate of tau^2 was too small and over-shrank; the unweighted method of moments estimate did better. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) Same risk for us: popular matchups (high n) are likely the balanced, meta ones, rare matchups (off-role picks) more extreme, so estimate tau^2 with an unweighted or method-of-moments estimator, not n-weighted. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f8]] [stats] Splitting into clearly different groups first (pitchers vs others) and shrinking within groups helps a lot; group means differ (0.255 vs 0.153). (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) For us the analogue of "group" is the additive champion baseline: shrink a matchup toward A's lead plus B's deficit, not toward the role mean. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[caafe-2023#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Acceptance rule: mean change in accuracy and ROC AUC over ten random validation splits, kept when the average of both is above 0; ten iterations per run, TabPFN as the fast evaluator. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f5]] [stats] The authors admit the keep rule is not a statistical test. `conclusion` (B/fair, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f1]] [stats] With large enough samples any minuscule difference becomes significant; fixing a minimally relevant effect size (MRES) before the experiment avoids reading that as practical superiority, yet it is rarely done. `1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Design the experiment to have the desired power at the MRES; smaller effects are by definition uninteresting, so missing them costs nothing. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f3]] [stats] Power rises with alpha, N and the standardised effect d = delta / sigma of the paired differences. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f4]] [stats] Fixing the MRES also adds a practical-relevance dimension to significance tests, beyond the p-value. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f5]] [stats] The independent units are the per-instance paired differences, not the individual runs; treating runs as independent is pseudoreplication with inflated degrees of freedom. `4.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f6]] [stats] Required N is the smallest N whose paired t-test reaches the target power at the MRES; computed iteratively, available in R. `4.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f7]] [stats] [lab] Their procedure checks first whether the needed N exceeds the instances available, and if so investigates power or changes parameters before running. `algorithm 2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f8]] [stats] Sequential testing (add samples and retest until significant) without correcting alpha inflates type I errors; they flag prior work for this. `1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f9]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: with sigma_d the SD of per-game log-loss differences between candidate and current model (clustered by game, as the harness already does), the two-sided 80% power MDE at alpha 0.05 is about 2.8 * sigma_d / sqrt(N_eff); an idea whose plausible gain is below th... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f1]] [stats] [lab] Overfitting the model selection criterion can cost as much as the difference between learning algorithms, so it cannot be ignored when comparing. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Low variance in the selection criterion matters at least as much as unbiasedness: variance is what lets selection overfit. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f3]] [stats] [lab] Demo (1000 realisations, 64 samples, four-fold CV): the CV estimate keeps falling while true test error rises again after about 30-40 iterations of tuning, the classic overfitting curve one level up. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f4]] [stats] [lab] It is worst with small data and many tuned choices; remedies are the same as for training overfitting: regularisation, early stopping, averaging, fewer hyperparameters, or for very small data integrate them out (Bayesian). Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f5]] [stats] [lab] Reported performance is optimistically biased unless model selection is redone inside each evaluation trial (nested cross-validation). Section 1, 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f6]] [stats] [lab] Rigorous evaluation uses multiple randomised partitionings with selection repeated in each, which is easy to automate and parallelise. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f1]] [method] [stats] Augmentation is averaging over the orbits of a symmetry group that keeps the data distribution (approximately) invariant, and it reduces variance. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Under only approximate invariance, augmentation adds bias as it cuts variance, so it may not help; the trade-off depends on the distance between the data and its transformed copy. Section 1, item 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Augmented maximum likelihood cannot always match a model where the invariance is built in by constraint. Section 1, item 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f1]] [tools] [stats] Raw matchup rates are biased by each side's overall strength; they subtract the opponent's average rate, then the hero's average normalised rate, and show the remainder (the pair effect) re-centred on 50%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f2]] [tools] [stats] Worked example: a raw 48% matchup becomes about 51% after removing both heroes' overall strength, i.e. the sign flips. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f3]] [stats] Selection bias from context: a hero picked into strong comps looks worse in raw matchup numbers than the matchup really is. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f4]] [stats] Survivorship in weighting: countered heroes get swapped out, so playtime weighting would hide the counter; each pair counts as 1 instead. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no minimum sample, shrinkage or Bayesian adjustment is described in this post (a search summary claimed shrinkage; the page text does not say so). (C/fair, absence)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) Two sequential mean-subtractions are an approximation of the additive two-way fit; with unbalanced counts (popular vs rare opponents) a proper additive fit (alternating means, i.e. backfitting) is better and just as cheap. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftcompromise#^f2]] [tools] [stats] Tools were close together, and within margins of error none recommended compositions that statistically win more than 50%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f7]] [stats] [method] Matchup expectation is additive on an Elo-like logit scale: expected rating = rating(A's role win rate) - rating(B's role win rate), with rating(w) = -400 log10(1/w - 1). `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts`, `packages/core/src/rating/ratings.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f8]] [stats] [method] [ux/uncertainty] Thin matchups are shrunk with pseudo-games: priorGames games at the additive expected win rate are added to the observed wins and games, then the matchup's rating is reported as the deviation from that expectation. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f9]] [stats] Both directions of a matchup are averaged (A's wins vs B, B's losses vs A) before shrinking. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f10]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] Prior strength is a user setting ("risk level"): 3000, 2000, 1000 (medium), 500 or 250 pseudo-games; fixed constants, not estimated from data. `packages/core/src/risk/risk-level.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) A 1000-game prior means a matchup needs about 1000 games before its own data counts as much as the additive baseline: far stronger than Lolalytics' 100 game display floor ([[lolalytics-counters-threshold#^f1]]), and close to what Tango's k = luck variance / talent variance give... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Reusing a holdout adaptively overfits the holdout itself; reporting the best of many tested predictors is the well-known cause. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Static multiple-testing corrections only work when the set of tested hypotheses is fixed in advance; adaptive choice (hyperparameter tuning, analyst in the loop, stopping conditions) breaks them. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Synthetic demo: with labels independent of data (true accuracy 50%), standard holdout reuse reported over 63% on both training and holdout for k = 500; a fresh set showed the truth. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Thresholdout: if training and holdout scores agree within a noisy threshold, return the training score; otherwise return the holdout score plus noise and spend one unit of an overfitting budget B; stop answering when B runs out. Section 4.1, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The number of queries Thresholdout can answer is exponential in holdout size n, as long as overfitting answers stay at most quadratic in n. Section 1.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f6]] [lab] [stats] SparseValidate: the analyst only gets a bit back per query, with budgets for total queries m and for failed validations B; information leaks only on failures. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Cross-validation and bootstrap do not fix adaptive reuse; before this the only known safe approach was a fresh holdout for each dependent validation. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f8]] [lab] [stats] With a real signal (20 informative attributes) Thresholdout still finds a good classifier while preventing overfitting: the guard does not block true discoveries. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f9]] [lab] [stats] The experiment used T = 0.04, below the values the proof needs, and Gaussian instead of Laplace noise: practical settings are empirical. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f1]] [stats] [method] P-splines: many equally spaced basis functions plus a difference penalty on neighbouring coefficients; smoothness is set by the penalty weight alone. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f2]] [stats] Large lambda tends to a straight line (second-order penalty) or a quadratic (third order); d = 2 or 3 is usual. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f3]] [stats] [method] For data already on an equally spaced discrete grid, use the identity basis: one value per point, a banded system; this is Whittaker's 1923 smoother for life tables. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f4]] [stats] The discrete smoother is fast even for 100,000 points, including leave-one-out cross-validation (Eilers 2003). (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f5]] [stats] Gaps with no data are interpolated smoothly by the penalty (zero weights); extrapolation past the data continues as a polynomial of lower degree. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f6]] [stats] [method] Lambda need not be hand-tuned: in the mixed-model view it is the ratio of residual variance to the variance of the differences, re-estimated in a few iterations. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f7]] [stats] Alternatively choose the penalty by AIC or cross-validation against the effective dimension. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f8]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve: y_t = mean diff at minute t, weight w_t = n_t / s_t^2 (games alive at t over within-minute variance); solve (W + lambda D2'D2) z = W y, a pentadiagonal system of ~30 unknowns. Thin late minutes get small weight and follow the smooth trend instead o... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) Smoothing across minutes and shrinkage across matchups are the same idea (penalised least squares with a variance-ratio penalty), which makes one coherent story for the method page. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f1]] [method] [stats] Window size trade-off: a short window adapts fast but hurts in stable periods; a long window is better when stable but reacts slowly. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f2]] [method] [stats] Recency is not relevance when data is noisy or concepts recur; slow change longer than the window also defeats windowing. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f3]] [method] [stats] Abrupt forgetting raises reactivity but also the risk of fitting noise. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f4]] [method] [stats] Gradual forgetting keeps all examples and weights them by age, e.g. exponential w = exp(-lambda j), lambda = 0 being equal weights. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f1]] [stats] [method] Treat every ANOVA row (main effects, interactions) as a batch of random effects with mean 0 and a standard deviation estimated from data. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f2]] [stats] Coefficients are pulled toward zero by an amount set by the batch's variance component; batches with near-zero variance are shrunk most. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f3]] [stats] Classical (point estimate of variances) and Bayesian inference agree given the variances; the difference matters only when a variance is hard to tell from 0. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f4]] [stats] Setting such a variance to zero gives falsely precise estimates; near-zero variances are expected for higher-order interactions. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For us: the matchup interaction gamma_AB (beyond A's average lead and B's average deficit) is the batch to shrink; if its estimated variance per role and minute is small, most thin matchups should collapse to the additive prediction. Floor tau^2 above zero (or report i... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f1]] [stats] An interaction's standard error is about twice a main effect's at the same sample size (four cells of N/4 against two of N/2). (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f2]] [stats] If interactions are half the size of main effects, a study with 80% power for the main effect has 10% power for the interaction. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f3]] [stats] Under low power, the estimates that reach significance overstate the true effect about threefold (2.4 vs 0.7 standard errors). (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f4]] [stats] Take-home: 16 times the sample for an interaction; interactions found by looking after the main analysis are mostly noise. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f1]] [stats] [method] Tango's method: talent variance = observed variance between teams minus the theoretical luck variance; luck from the binomial. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f2]] [stats] [method] The regression constant k is the number of games where luck SD equals talent SD; add k games of league-average performance to every record. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f3]] [stats] k does not depend on the sample size: the same pseudo-games are added to an 8-game or a 162-game record; k = within-unit variance / talent variance. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f4]] [stats] Weight on the observed value is t^2/v^2, the reliability (r-squared of outcome on talent). (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f5]] [stats] Gelman: this is hierarchical Bayes with a point estimate of the group-level variance, as in BDA chapter 2. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f6]] [stats] Insurance credibility theory (Buhlmann) uses the same N/(N+K) weight, K the ratio of within- to between-individual variance. Comment (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve at minute t: k_t = sigma_t^2 / tau_t^2, where sigma_t^2 is the within-matchup game-to-game variance of gold diff at t and tau_t^2 the between-matchup variance of true interaction effects; estimate = (n * observed + k_t * baseline) / (n + k_t). This ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f1]] [method] [stats] Generalisation error falls as a power law in training-set size; empirical exponents sit between -0.07 and -0.35, flatter than the -0.5 theory predicts. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f3]] [method] [stats] Three regions: best guessing at small data, then power-law scaling, then a floor at irreducible (Bayes) error. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f4]] [method] [stats] A counting model for a weighted coin flip converges with exponent -0.5, the textbook rate. Section 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f6]] [stats] (inferred) For a win model the floor is huge: Bayes log loss is near 0.69 because outcomes are mostly noise. Fit the power law to excess loss over the floor (or to AUC), with the floor as a free parameter, else extrapolation is meaningless; per f2, a better model buys a shift, while more ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hyperband#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Telling two candidates apart needs more resource when partial results are noisier or their final scores are closer. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f7]] [lab] [stats] On 117 data sets, Bayesian methods won on validation rank by more than on test rank, a sign of overfitting the validation set; random search at twice the budget beat all. Section 4.2.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f2]] [method] [stats] CANN on a GBM base was lowest deviance for frequency on all four sets; the plain FFNN was the one model slightly worse on the low-feature set. 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f4]] [stats] Evaluation is time-split: only games before the predicted period are used for training. (D/fair, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f1]] [method] [stats] Draft-only on patch 16.6 test (15,092 games, trained on 16.1-16.5): every model lands at AUC about 0.554 and 53.5-53.8% accuracy; log loss 0.6883-0.6894 vs constant 0.6930. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.1 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f5]] [method] [stats] Symmetry built in: score = side + h(blue, red) - h(red, blue), so swapping teams flips the probability; XGBoost gets both orientations as training rows instead. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f6]] [stats] Learning curves have not levelled off at 73k training games; improvement over constant roughly 1.3, 2.0, 2.5, 4.0 (x1000) at 9k, 18k, 37k, 73k games for strength. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.2 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f7]] [data] [stats] Recency weighting by patch age: strong down-weighting (gamma 0.5 or less per patch) hurts, 0.7 about equals no weighting. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.3 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f8]] [data] [stats] Leak: player season win rates fetched after the games include the predicted game; with them the first transformer reached about 63%, and a one-line higher-team-win-rate rule 62.4%. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 3 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f10]] [stats] Calibration fine but predictions narrow: about two thirds between 45% and 55%, 95% between 40% and 60%. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f11]] [stats] With 15k test games, log loss differences below about 0.0005 are indistinguishable from noise. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 10 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f19]] [method] [stats] Selected configs: FM k=4, lam 20, best at epoch 14; transformer lam 1, best at epoch 2, i.e. the transformer barely moved from the LR warm start before validation loss turned. `outputs/model_comparison/configs.json` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f20]] [stats] Protocol: tune on train with validation = latest 40% (per platform, by time) of the second-latest patch, then refit on train + validation for the chosen number of epochs and score the latest patch. `src/draft_features.py`, `scripts/compare_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f21]] [method] [stats] Test log loss (patch 16.6): LR strength 0.68851, LR with pair one-hots 0.68834, FM 0.68861, transformer 0.68847, XGBoost 0.68943; accuracy 53.5%, 53.6%, 53.8%, 53.6%, 53.6%. `outputs/model_comparison/results.csv` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jihan-yin-champ-select#^f2]] [stats] (inferred) With two rows per match and a random row split, a match's mirror can land in train while the other side is in test, a duplicate-style leak. (D/fair, unquoted)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f11]] [lab] [stats] 9 of 12 papers had no significance test or uncertainty for classifier comparison; one claimed win had a bootstrap AUC interval of [0.66-0.95]. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f1]] [method] [stats] Many delicate shifts happen at once, each with few instances; time windows and instance decay lose too much signal by discarding data. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f2]] [method] [stats] Measured: across exponential decay rates on two model families, quality improved as decay was moderated, best with no decay at all. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f3]] [method] [stats] Why: old data still carries persistent and cross-entity patterns, so down-weighting it loses signal with the noise when data per entity is scarce. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f1]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] Readers misjudge error bars: asked for the separation needed for P = 0.05, only 22% were within a factor of 2. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f2]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] s.d. bars show the spread of the population and predict the range of new samples; they are not the error in the mean. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f3]] [stats] s.e.m. = s.d. / sqrt(n) shows uncertainty in the mean and shrinks with n. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f4]] [stats] 95% CI is about 2 s.e.m. for n > 15; a CI is more intuitive than s.e.m. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f5]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] [ux/charts] Always state which bar type is shown. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f6]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] (inferred) For players the useful band is the game-to-game spread ("in half of games you'll be between X and Y gold at 10"), i.e. percentiles of the observed diffs, labelled as such; the uncertainty of the mean belongs in n, the grey-out and a thin CI line or text, not as... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f1]] [stats] Partly conditional summaries are cross-sectional means among those still observed at each time point, describing the dynamic cohort of survivors, not individual trajectories. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f2]] [stats] Unconditional models (e.g. random effects on unbalanced data) implicitly impute values after death, describing an "immortal cohort"; valid only if deaths do not happen or are independent of the response. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f3]] [stats] Fully conditional models stratify trajectories by time of death. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f4]] [stats] Joint models describe the whole cohort at each time point with a combined outcome (alive and healthy), so data stay balanced. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f5]] [stats] The choice must match the research aim. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f6]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: the honest default is partly conditional (plain mean of games still running at minute t), labelled "games still running"; do not smooth or model across minutes in a way that imputes ended games (a smoother weighted by n_t is fine, it only borrows ac... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f3]] [stats] Reported log loss about 0.68 after tuning (constant 0.693); most common comps sit at 45-55% win; coefficients stable across CV folds. (C/fair, verified)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no accuracy, AUC, split method or patch window. (C/fair, absence)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f2]] [stats] Evaluation: 5-fold cross-validation (random, not by time), neural approximators trained 100 epochs with Adam. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f5]] [method] [stats] The authors read LoL as overfitting: draft counter-picking and the huge composition space make unseen matchups hard; more games needed. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f1]] [stats] In LoL three components predict outcomes: champion base skill, player base skill and player-specific champion skill; in Dota 2 mostly champion base skill. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f2]] [stats] Adding player-champion terms gave the best accuracy in LoL. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f3]] [stats] (inferred) So for a thin matchup where a few one-tricks supply many of the games, game-level standard errors understate uncertainty; count distinct players and cap or down-weight games per player. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[lol-wiki-surrender#^f5]] [data] [stats] (inferred) So curves to minute 14 are close to a full sample (only AFK games end early, and those should be dropped anyway); from 15 on, games that are lost badly drop out, and the surviving games at minute 20+ are biased toward closer lanes. Curves past 15 must be read as "among g... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[loldraftai#^f19]] [data] [stats] Train and test drop games where any player has under 2400 gold, over 10 deaths, under 25 CS (non-support) or under level 4 at 15 minutes, which also removes every game ending before 15:00. `prepare_data.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f20]] [stats] (inferred) Those filters use in-game information unknown in champ select and remove stomps and AFK games from the test set too, so reported accuracy is on easier, cleaner games than the tool sees live. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[loldraftai#^f21]] [stats] Default split is a seeded random 90/10 by export file, not by time; an optional next-patch mode tests on the 2 newest patches mapped to the third newest. `prepare_data.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f23]] [stats] The checkpoint kept is the epoch with best loss on the same test split that reports accuracy, so the test set doubles as validation. `train.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f24]] [stats] Reported solo queue figures: win log loss 0.684 after the fix (constant 0.693) and calibration buckets over about 783k validation games, 50-55% predicted bucket 428,768 games at 0.525 actual. `correction-reddit-post/page.tsx` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f25]] [stats] The duplication bug had made true accuracy 52% while claims were higher; README later states loss 0.681, accuracy 56.1% at epoch 46. `correction-reddit-post/page.tsx`, `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f26]] [tools] [stats] The DraftGap vs LoLDraftAI accuracy comparison was withdrawn because validation data leaked into training. `draftgap-vs-loldraftai-comparison/page.tsx` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f31]] [method] [stats] Checked, not there: no early stopping; all 50 epochs run, the best-validation-loss state is saved each time it improves and a timestamped checkpoint every epoch. `train.py` (C/fair, absence)
- [[loldraftai#^f34]] [stats] Validation runs on both the plain and a masked copy of the test split every epoch; `validation.py` (subgroup losses by elo, patch and champion play rate, quantile ECE, matchup calibration) is marked as out of date for the current architecture. `train.py`, `validation.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-item-recs#^f3]] [stats] Item win rates are biased by purchase context: win-more items look strong, desperation items (Zhonya's) look weak, one-trick items look good. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-item-recs#^f4]] [stats] (claim) Its model adjusts for gold difference, player champion skill and enemy matchups to isolate an item's effect. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-recommendations#^f1]] [tools] [stats] The team comp analyzer adjusts win rates for champion experience, either from your own ranked history or a fixed 25 games per champion. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-recommendations#^f2]] [stats] Example: Gragas players averaged far fewer games on him than Shaco players, worth 1.6% win rate. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Evaluate stochastic models several times: different seeds at least, better also varied data (CV); keep a subject's rows in one fold; report spread (SD or 95% CI). 4.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f11]] [lab] [stats] Comparing two model instances by per-sample outputs (McNemar) needs the per-sample predictions recorded. 5.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f12]] [stats] Many pairwise tests multiply false positives (20 tests at 95%: one likely wrong); correct, e.g. Bonferroni. 5.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lsreg-score-screening#^f1]] [stats] [method] The score test is computed from the base fit only, for screening many candidate covariates; no parameter estimate is produced. `Overview` (C/fair, verified)
- [[lsreg-score-screening#^f2]] [stats] On simulated data the two true covariates of ten candidates had the largest score statistics, and values matched statmod's to numerical precision. `Results` (C/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f3]] [method] [stats] Window size is a trade-off: small reflects the latest distribution, large gives more training data. Section 5.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[meinmmo-loldraftai#^f1]] [method] [stats] Launch claim (2 Apr 2025 Reddit post): the AI predicts 62% of ranked matches. (D/weak, verified)
- [[meiolol-filters-prototype#^f3]] [render] [stats] Only summable numbers filter: model estimates (lane-curve gold at 14) and rates with other denominators (pick, ban) do not. (B/fair, verified)
- [[meiolol-matchup-counts#^f2]] [seo] [stats] All regions, last 30 days: 165 of 12,925 matchup pages have 200+ games, 635 have 100+, 2,576 have 30+. (B/fair, verified)
- [[meiolol-matchup-counts#^f3]] [seo] [stats] EUW, last 30 days: 12 of 7,508 have 200+ games, 54 have 100+. (B/fair, verified)
- [[metabot-garen-vs-darius#^f2]] [seo] [stats] It shows a 95% interval for the win rate, as meiolol's site does. (C/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Whether more data would help and how much data to use both reduce to locating the saturation point of the curve. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f4]] [lab] [stats] If performance is already near the data's intrinsic noise, more rows will not help; acquire new features instead. Section 4.1.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Naive stopping rules on noisy curves stop too early; confidence intervals are wide at small anchors and narrow with size, so choose the point where the interval is narrow enough. Section 5.1.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f1]] [method] [stats] Learning curves cross: logistic regression wins on small training sets, trees on large ones, often within the same domain, so model comparisons must look at the curve, not one size. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f2]] [method] [stats] Which wins at scale is predicted by signal separability: trees for well-separable data, logistic regression where classes separate poorly. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f3]] [method] [stats] Low separability (max AUC <= 0.8): when distinguishable, logistic regression won 8-1-1 on AUC; several sets were indistinguishable within error bars. Section 5.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f1]] [stats] Effective sample size is n divided by the design effect: the simple random sample size giving the same variance. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f2]] [stats] Kish's weighting design effect is 1 + relvariance of the weights, i.e. n_eff = (sum w)^2 / sum w^2; easy but derived under restrictive assumptions. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f3]] [stats] Clustering increases the design effect with within-cluster homogeneity rho and cluster size: deff = 1 + rho (n-bar - 1). (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f4]] [stats] Even a small positive rho matters when clusters are big. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: with patch-decay weights w_g, use n_eff = (sum w)^2 / sum w^2 in the shrinkage weight and the "n" shown to users; with players contributing m games each, n_eff ~ n / (1 + rho (m-bar - 1)). Estimate rho once per role from data (variance of player mea... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f4]] [method] [stats] Sparse data calls for a small latent size k: restricting expressiveness generalises better. Section III.A.2 (text split by column layout, no verbatim quote) (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f1]] [stats] Hard minimum-count filters throw away information; shrinkage is the alternative. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f2]] [stats] [method] Two steps: estimate the distribution of the quantity over all units, then use it as the prior for each unit. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f3]] [stats] [method] With a beta prior the update is pseudo-counts: add alpha0 to successes and alpha0 + beta0 to the total; one line of code. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f4]] [stats] Prior strength fitted from real data was about 300 pseudo at-bats for career batting averages: a 4/10 batter is estimated at 0.264, a 300/1000 batter at 0.29. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f5]] [stats] Shrinkage depends on evidence: little data moves a lot, much data barely. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f6]] [stats] Better priors depend on known factors (era, team, position), i.e. a hierarchical model; he recommends fitting the prior on all data weighted by evidence (beta-binomial ML) rather than a filtered subset. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup win rates: shrink toward the additive expectation p0 (from both champions' overall win rates) with pseudo-count m: (wins + m * p0) / (games + m), m fitted per role by method of moments. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) Plain-language line for our method page: "Extraordinary outliers require extraordinary evidence". (B/fair, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f1]] [stats] [method] Player gold is modelled as intercept + champion random effect + player random effect + noise, fitted as a Bayesian hierarchical model. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f2]] [stats] They chose hierarchical Bayes because it works with scarce and imbalanced data through shrinkage and pooling. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f3]] [stats] Heavy-tailed (Student t, 3 df) random effects so outlier champions or players are not over-shrunk. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f6]] [stats] Player and champion effects confound: most players stick to a few champions, so a model without a champion effect credits champion strength to the player. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f7]] [stats] Champion effects are stable across patches (concordance typically above 0.8 on EUW and NA). (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) For matchup curves: remove AFK or disconnect games before averaging (a lane "win" against an absent player is not the matchup); and one-trick players may dominate a rare champion's games, so a thin matchup may measure a few players, not the champions. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f1]] [data] [stats] 60k high-elo games, all on patch 10.16, sorted by time: first 35k train, next 10k validation, last 15k test. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f5]] [method] [stats] Test (last 15k games): champion-only 54.06%, with player features 53.45%, below its validation score; the author suspects overfitting from the extra features or drift over time. Section 7.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f1]] [stats] [method] Score tests need only the null model fitted; Wald needs the alternative, LRT needs both. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f2]] [stats] [lab] That makes them cheap for testing many candidate covariates against a simple null model. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f3]] [stats] The three tests are asymptotically equivalent in ideal conditions. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f4]] [stats] [method] The code computes it as a residual correlation: project the candidate off the current design (weighted QR residual), dot it with the weighted working residuals, divide by the projected candidate's norm (and dispersion, 1 for binomial). Result is a z-statistic, standard normal und... (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f5]] [stats] Limits: one numeric covariate per statistic; factors and multi-column terms need anova.glm's score option, which refits. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no estimate of the effect size or of the predictive gain (log-loss) the covariate would bring; only a test statistic. (A/strong, absence)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f7]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a candidate per-game feature (say a matchup term) can be screened as z = sum(x_resid * r) / norm(x_resid) on the current model's per-game residuals, in milliseconds, before any harness run; the z for a screen is not evidence of held-out gain and many screens ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] Fit error(N) = a N^-b + c (c the irreducible floor) to nested subsets at seven fractions (1% to 100%) on one fixed test set. Section 3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f2]] [method] [stats] Power laws fit well (R^2 > 0.8) on 77.7% of cells; tree ensembles win at full data, linear models underperform on classification. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f3]] [method] [stats] Logistic regression has the steepest stable exponent (median b = 0.83), boosting 0.48, random forest 0.33. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f4]] [method] [stats] Exponent alone misleads: what matters is exponent and floor together. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f6]] [stats] (inferred) Our curve (-0.0008 log loss per doubling) should be fitted with a floor c, at several nested fractions, and compared between model families on the same test set; a single exponent from one run is ±15% at best. (B/fair, unquoted)
